\Psi_{B,f,h}(S_{\alpha_{vI_{v}J_{v}},\epsilon})=(\prod_{\bar{v}\in V(S_{\alpha_{vI_{v}J_{v}},\epsilon})}f(\bar{v}))g_{S_{\alpha_{vI_{v}J_{v}},\epsilon},h}\gamma_{S_{\alpha_{vI_{v}J_{v}},\epsilon}}.

(4.22)

Note that
S_{\alpha_{vI_{v}J_{v}},\epsilon}
for fixed
\alpha_{vI_{v}J_{v}}
and for all small enough
\epsilon
has the same abstract graph structure and the same
\epsilon
independent vertex intertwiners.
It follows that
\gamma_{S_{\alpha_{vI_{v}J_{v}},\epsilon}}

is independent of
\epsilon
.
Next, note that from (
4.15
) we have that

g_{S_{\alpha_{vI_{v}J_{v}},\epsilon},h}=O(\epsilon^{2}).

(4.23)

Using the fact that
f
is a
C^{r}
function (so that its evaluations are bounded independent of
\epsilon
) together with (
4.23
) in (
4.22
) implies that for small enough
\epsilon
:

\Psi_{B,f,h}(S_{\alpha_{vI_{v}J_{v}},\epsilon})=\frac{O(\epsilon^{2})}{\epsilon}=O(\epsilon)

(4.24)

which implies that the
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
limit of (
4.24
), and, hence of (
4.21
) vanishes.

Next, let
S
be a spin network which has exclusively GR vertices so that
V(S)
comprises only of (not necessarily nondegenerate) GR vertices.
We have that:

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)S(A):=\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v\in V_{GR}(S)}N(x(v))\big(\;\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{([\frac{1}{{}^{N_{v}-1}\!P_{3}}\sum_{\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}}S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}]-{\frac{1}{4}}S_{\lambda_{v}})}{\epsilon}

\displaystyle-(\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}\sum_{J_{v}\neq I_{v}}\sum_{\beta_{vI_{v}J_{v}}}P_{\beta_{vI_{v}J_{v}}}\frac{S_{\beta_{vI_{v}J_{v}},\epsilon}}{\epsilon})\;\big)

(4.25)

where we remind the reader that
V_{GR}
is the set of all
nondegenerate
GR vertices in
V(S)
.
Note that if
S
does not have overlap in
B
, none of the states in the first line of (
4.25
) have overlap in
B
. To see this recall from section
3.4.2
that if
S

has no overlap in
B
and has an expansion (
3.9
), none of the asymmetric spin network basis states in this expansion are in
B
.
Thus, if
\{S_{i},i=1,..,n\}
are the set of these states (with non-zero expansion coefficients),
we have that
S_{i}\notin B,\;i=1,..,n
. If

S_{i}\notin B
, none of its children are in
B

else
S_{i}
would be a possible parent, by electric diffeomorphism, of a child in
B
and hence, itself in
B
by
(iii), section
3.2
. However, equation (
3.21
) shows that every electric diffeomorphism
child of
S
is a linear combination of children of
\{S_{i},i=1,..,n\}
. This shows that no electric diffeomorphism
child of
S
in the first line of (
4.25
) can have overlap in
B
. Note also that (
3.19
) shows that

S_{\lambda_{v}}
also cannot have overlap in
B
if
S
has no overlap in
B
.
It follows that none of the states in the first line of (
4.25
) can have any overlap with states in
B
, if
S
has no overlap in
B
but admits the expansion (
3.9
).

Next, let
S
not admit such an expansion.
Then it must be the case that the embedded colored graph underlying
S
is distinct from that underlying
any asymmetric spin net basis element so that
S
necessarily cannot have overlap in
B
by virtue of the fact that every element of
B
is such an asymmetric spin net basis element (see (i), section
3.2
).
Since
S_{\lambda_{v}}
lives on the same colored decorated graph as
S
, it also does not admit such an expansion and hence also does not have overlap in
B
.
Similarly if
S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}
also does not admit an expansion of type (
3.9
), its decorated colored graph is also distinct from that underlying any asymmetric spin network basis element and hence

S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}
does not have overlap in
B
.
Next, recall again that in any expansion of a spin network in terms of other spin networks, all spin networks contributing non-trivially to the expansion necessarily live on the same
embedded colored graph.
Suppose now that
S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}
does admit an expansion (
3.9
) so that its embedded colored graph is a permissible embedding of some abstract decorated graph
\gamma^{\prime}_{m,c,d}
which necessarily has (at least) a triple of
appropriately placed bivalent kinks
in its non-loop
component. Clearly
S
must live on an embedded colored graph obtained by removing a triple of kinks from the non-loop component of that underlying
S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}
, which implies that
the embedded colored graph underlying
S
is a permissible embedding of the abstract decorated graph obtained by removing an appropriate triple of kinks from edges in the non-loop component of
\gamma^{\prime}_{m,c,d}
.
Since such a graph is of type
\gamma_{m,c,d}
or
\gamma^{\prime}_{m,c,d}
,
S
necessarily admits an expansion in terms of embedded spin networks each of which is an embedding of an element of

{\bf E}_{\gamma_{m,c}}
. Thus
S

must admit
an expansion of type (
3.9
) which is a contradiction so that
S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}
cannot have overlap in
B
.
Thus, if
S
does not have overlap in
B
, none of the states in the first line of (
4.25
) can have any overlap in
B
, which proves the assertion.
As a result, the contribution from the first line of (
4.25
)
vanishes.

Next, consider the ‘propagation’ contributions in the second line of (
4.25
).
These contributions can be seen vanish in the continuum limit by
arguments identical to those which lead to (
4.23
), (
4.24
)

whether or not
S
has overlap
in
B
. More in detail for any nondegenerate vertex
v
in
S
, we have, once again, that:

g_{S_{\alpha_{vI_{v}J_{v}},\epsilon},h}=O(\epsilon^{2}),\;\;\;\Psi_{B,f,h}(S_{\alpha_{vI_{v}J_{v}},\epsilon})=\frac{O(\epsilon^{2})}{\epsilon}=O(\epsilon).

(4.26)

so that the continuum limit of the propagation part of the constraint vanishes.

As a result, the amplitude (
4.16
) for
S
with no overlap in
B
vanishes in its continuum limit (
4.17
)
and the only possibly non-trivial contributions to this continuum limit amplitude for
S
which has overlap
in
B
arise solely from the first line of (
4.25
).
Therefore, in (
4.25
) we restrict attention to
S
which
has overlap in
B
and consider contributions only from its first line. From arguments similar to those above
it then follows that all the states in the first
line of
(
4.25
) have overlap in
B
so that the contributions of interest evaluate to:

\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)S)=\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v\in V_{GR}(s)}N(x(v))\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{([\frac{1}{{}^{N_{v}-1}\!P_{3}}\sum_{\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}}\Psi_{B,f,h}(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\epsilon})]-{\frac{1}{4}}\Psi_{B,f,h}(S_{\lambda_{v}}))}{\epsilon}.

(4.27)

Omitting the subscript ‘
h
’ on the interkink distance function
g
, we have that:

\displaystyle\Psi_{B,f,h}(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\epsilon})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v_{I,\epsilon}}f(v^{\prime})f(v_{I,\epsilon})g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v},\epsilon}}\gamma_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v},\epsilon}}

(4.28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v}f(v^{\prime})f(v_{I_{v},\epsilon})(\frac{1}{4}g_{S})\gamma_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}+O(\epsilon^{2})

where we have used (
3.11
) in the first line and
(
4.1
) and (
3.22
) in the second line of (
4.28
). Here, in the first line
v^{\prime}
ranges over all vertices of
S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v},\epsilon}
other than
v_{I,\epsilon}
. Equivalently,
in the second line
v^{\prime}
ranges over all elements of
S
other than the parental vertex of interest,
v
.

Next, note that in the RNC’s at
v
we have that

x^{\mu}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon})=x^{\mu}(v)+\epsilon{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{\mu}+O(\epsilon^{2})

(4.29)

where
{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{\mu}
is the unit (with respect to the metric
h
) tangent to the
I_{v}
th edge at
v
.
To see this, note that
I_{v}
th edge is a
C^{r},r>>1
curve and hence a
C^{r-1}
curve in terms of the
C^{r-1}
RNCs at
v
.
In a
C^{r-1}
parameterization by some parameter
t
such that
v
lies at parameter
t=0
and
v_{I,\epsilon}
at parameter
t_{\epsilon}
, Taylor expansion yields

x^{\mu}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon})-x^{\mu}(v)=\frac{dx^{\mu}}{dt}(t=0)t_{\epsilon}+O(t_{\epsilon}^{2}).

(4.30)

Since
v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
lies at an RNC coordinate distance
\epsilon
from
v
and since the metric at
v
in RNCs is
h_{\mu\nu}=\delta_{\mu\nu}
we have that

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu=1}^{3}(x^{\mu}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon})-x^{\mu}(v))^{2}

(4.31)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(t_{\epsilon})^{2}(\delta_{\mu\nu}(\frac{dx^{\mu}}{dt}(t=0)\frac{dx^{\nu}}{dt}(t=0))+O(t_{\epsilon}))

(4.32)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\epsilon

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf O}(t_{\epsilon})\Rightarrow O(t_{\epsilon})=O(\epsilon)

(4.33)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\epsilon+O(\epsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\epsilon}(h_{\mu\nu}\frac{dx^{\mu}}{dt}(t=0)\frac{dx^{\nu}}{dt}(t=0))^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.34)

where we substituted (
4.33
) in (the square root of) (
4.32
) to obtain (
4.34
). Using (
4.34
) in (
4.30
) together with the fact that

\frac{dx^{\mu}}{dt}(t=0)
is the
I_{v}
th edge tangent in the parameterization
t
yields the desired result (
4.29
).
Note that by virtue of the defining property of RNCs the unit edge tangent with respect to the metric
h
agrees with the unit coordinate edge tangent (see also (
2.19
)).
Using the fact that
f
is
C^{r}
, (
4.29
) implies that:

f(v_{I_{v},\epsilon})=f(v)+\epsilon{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f(v)+O(\epsilon^{2}).

(4.35)

Using this in conjunction with (
4.28
) and (
3.22
) we have that:

\displaystyle{[\frac{1}{{}^{N_{v}-1}\!P_{3}}\sum_{\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}}\Psi_{B,f,h}(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\epsilon})]-{\frac{1}{4}}\Psi_{B,f,h}(S_{\lambda_{v}})}

(4.36)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\;\;(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v}f(v^{\prime}))\;\frac{1}{4}g_{S}\gamma_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}(f(v)+\epsilon{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{\mu}\partial f(v)\;+\;\;O(\epsilon^{2}))\;\;\big)-\big((\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v}f(v^{\prime}))\frac{1}{4}g_{S}\gamma_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}f(v)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\;\frac{1}{4}g_{S}\gamma_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v}f(v^{\prime})){\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f(v)\;\big)\;\epsilon\;\;+\;\;O(\epsilon^{2})

from which it follows that:

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)S)=\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v\in V_{GR}(s)}N(x(v)g_{S}\gamma_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v}f(v^{\prime}))(\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f(v))

(4.37)

where we remind the reader that the vertex set
V(S)
of
S
has only GR vertices, that

V_{GR}(S)
is the set of nondegenerate vertices of
S
and that in (
4.37
),
v^{\prime}
ranges over all elements of
V(S)
other than
v
.
To summarise, the continuum limit of the single action of the Hamiltonian constraint on the off shell basis state
\Psi_{B,f,h}
is given by the following exhaustive set of
amplitudes:

\displaystyle\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}(N)S)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)S)

(4.38)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;\;{\rm if}\;\;S\;{\rm has}\;{\rm no}\;{\rm overlap}\;{\rm in}\;B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v\in V_{GR}(s)}N(x(v)g_{S}\gamma_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\in V(S),v^{\prime}\neq v}f(v^{\prime}))(\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v))\;\;

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\ \;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\ \;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;{\rm if}\;\;S\;{\rm has}\;{\rm overlap}\;{\rm in}\;B

(4.39)

where
we have replaced the coordinate index
\mu
by an abstract index to
emphasize the coordinate independence of the derivative of
f
along the unit (with respect to
h
)
I_{v}
th edge tangent at
v
.

4.3
Constraint Product and Commutator

The (dual) action of a product of Hamiltonian constraints at parameters
{\bar{\epsilon}},\epsilon
on
\Psi_{B,f,h}
is defined through the evaluations, for all spin networks
S
, of the amplitude:

\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M){\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)S).

(4.40)

The continuum limit action of the product of Hamiltonian constraints on
\Psi_{B,f,h}
, is defined to be the
{\bar{\epsilon}},\epsilon\rightarrow 0
limit of the evaluations, for all
S
, of the amplitude (
4.40
):

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}(\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M){\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)S)).

(4.41)

The commutator between a pair of Hamiltonian constraints can then be inferred directly from (
4.41
) to be

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}(\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M){\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)S))-\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}(\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(N){\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(M)S)).

(4.42)

In section
4.3.1
we show that the continuum limit of the constraint product amplitude (
4.41
) vanishes for
S
which has one or more NGR vertices.
In section
4.3.2
we evaluate the continuum limit of the constraint product amplitude for
S
which has exclusively GR vertices.
In section
4.3.3
we infer the commutator (
4.42
) from the evaluation of (
4.41
) in sections
4.3.1
and
4.3.2
.

4.3.1
Vanishing Product Amplitude for the case that
S
has NGR vertices

We have that:

{\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M){\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)S={\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)({\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)S|_{V_{GR}(S)})+{\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)({\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)S|_{V_{NGR}(S)}).

(4.43)

The first contribution above, from nondegenerate GR vertices in
S
results in spin networks which we denote by
\{S_{\chi,\epsilon}\}
for some appropriate enumeration index
\chi
.
From (
4.38
) the
\bar{\epsilon}\rightarrow 0
limit of the amplitude
\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)(S_{\chi,\epsilon}))
vanishes
because every
S_{\chi,\epsilon}
has NGR vertices where
{\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)
has not acted.
The same argument shows that the contribution of the second term in (
4.43
) to the continuum limit in
\bar{\epsilon}
also vanishes if there are 2 or more NGR vertices in
S
.

Consider the remaining case, in which
S
has a single
NGR
vertex at
v_{0}
.
Let
v_{0}
be nondegenerate (else there is no contribution to the second term of (
4.43
) so that both terms on the right hand side of (
4.43
) vanish).
From section
2.4
, the diffeomorphism part of the action of
{\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)
at
v_{0}
results in spin nets which continue to have a single
NGR vertex. It follows that the
\bar{\epsilon}
continuum limit vanishes on these spin networks. Hence we only need to consider the propagation type children generated by
{\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)
at
v_{0}
for which the propagation deformation
results in
v_{0}
changing from an NGR vertex in
S
to a GR vertex or a point in the interior of an edge
or to a (trivalent or bivalent) kink in the child. Amongst these children, from
(
4.38
), we only need to consider children which happen to have overlap in
B
. Let us denote such a child by
C_{\epsilon}
.
The continuum limit action of
{\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
on such a state is given by (
4.39
):

\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}(M)C_{\epsilon})=\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v\in V_{GR}(C_{\epsilon})}M(x(v))g_{C_{\epsilon}}\gamma_{{C_{\epsilon}}_{\lambda_{v}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\in V(C_{\epsilon}),v^{\prime}\neq v}f(v^{\prime}))(\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f(v)).

(4.44)

Note that none of the vertices of
C_{\epsilon}
change their positions as
\epsilon
decreases, only the kinks contract.
Also note that
\gamma_{{C_{\epsilon}}_{\lambda_{v}}}
only depends on the colored graph structure and vertex intertwiners of
C_{\epsilon}
and these are independent of
\epsilon
.
Thus
\gamma_{{C_{\epsilon}}_{\lambda_{v}}}
is independent of
\epsilon
. The only
\epsilon
dependent contribution to (
4.44
) is then

g_{C_{\epsilon}}
. From (
4.15
) this contribution is of
O(\epsilon^{2})
for small enough
\epsilon
.
Since the propagation children generated from an NGR vertex come with a factor of
\epsilon^{-1}
(see (
2.29
), we have that:

\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}(M){\frac{C_{\epsilon}}{\epsilon}})=O(\epsilon)

(4.45)

which implies that the contribution of the second term of (
4.43
) to the continuum limit product amplitude in (
4.41
) vanishes because it involves the double limit in which
the
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
limit is taken after the
\bar{\epsilon}\rightarrow 0
limit.

To summarise: the continuum limit product amplitude vanishes for spin networks
S
with at least one NGR vertex.
Accordingly, in the next section we focus on the case in which
S
has exclusively GR vertices.

4.3.2
Product Amplitude for the case of exclusively GR vertices

Consider the continuum limit product amplitude (
4.41
)
in the case that
S
has exclusively GR vertices.
First consider the propagation type states generated by the action
of
{\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)
on such
S
. It is immediate to see that the arguments of section
4.3.1
for the contribution of such propagation children continue to hold if the vertex
v_{0}

in those arguments is GR in
S
instead of NGR in
S
. Hence if the propagation part of action of
{\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)
on
S
results children with overlap in
B
,
the contribution of such propagation children to the product amplitude vanishes in the
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
continuum limit. If such propagation children do not have overlap in
B
, (
4.38
) implies that the action of
{\hat{H}}(M)

on such children vanishes. Hence the only remaining contributions to the product amplitude arise from the action of the diffeomorphism part of
{\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)
on
S
i.e from the states in the first line of (
4.25
).
First, consider the case in which
S
has no overlap with
B
. Recall from the arguments immediately after (
4.25
) that if
S
has exclusively GR vertices and has no overlap with
B
, none of the states in the first line of (
4.25
) have overlap in
S
. Equation (
4.38
) then implies that the contribution of the electric diffeomorphism part of
{\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)
to the product amplitude vanishes for such
S
.
It follows that the continuum limit product amplitude vanishes for
S
with no overlap in
B
and for
S
with overlap in
B
only the children generated by the diffeomorphism part of
{\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)
i.e. the states
in the first line of (
4.25
)contribute to this
amplitude. Recall, from the discussion after (
4.25
) that for
S
with overlap in
B
all the states in the
first line of (
4.25
) have overlap in
B
.
From these arguments together with (
2.34
) it follows that for
S
with overlap in
B
:

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}(\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M){\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)S))\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\big(\lim_{\bar{\epsilon}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}\left({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v\in V_{GR}(S)}N(x(v))\;\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{([\frac{1}{{}^{N_{v}-1}\!P_{3}}\sum_{\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}}S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}]-{\frac{1}{4}}S_{\lambda_{v}})}{\epsilon}\right)\big)

(4.46)

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v\in V_{GR}(S)}N(x(v))\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\;\left(\frac{[\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{1}{{}^{N_{v}-1}\!P_{3}}\sum_{\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}}\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})]-N_{v}{\frac{1}{4}}\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v}})}{\epsilon}\right)

(4.47)

where in the last line we have used the independence of
\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v}})
from the label
I_{v}
to replace the sum over
I_{v}
by a factor of
N_{v}
.

From (
4.39
) we have that:

\displaystyle\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})=:\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{\bar{v}\in V_{GR}(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})}M(x_{\bar{v}}(\bar{v}))g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})_{\lambda_{\bar{v}}}}(\prod_{\bar{v}^{\prime}\neq\bar{v}}f(\bar{v}^{\prime}))(\sum_{\bar{I}_{\bar{v}}=1}^{N_{\bar{v}}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{\bar{I}_{\bar{v}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(\bar{v})).

(4.48)

Here
\bar{v},\bar{v}^{\prime}
refer to vertices of the state
S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}
. The vertex label
\bar{v}
ranges over the set of nondegenerate (necessarily GR) vertices
V_{GR}(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})

and can either be
v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
or any of the undeformed nondegenerate vertices of
S
(i.e nondegenerate vertices other than
v
).
The vertex label
\bar{v}^{\prime}
ranges over the entire vertex set of necessarily GR but not necessarily nondegenerate vertices,
V(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})
. The index

{\bar{I}}_{\bar{v}}
is an edge index for the edges emanating from
\bar{v}
in
S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}
.

In the argument of the lapse
M
we have found it useful to add an explicit subscript
\bar{v}
to the Riemann Normal Coordinate notation adopted so far.
Thus
\{x_{\bar{v}}\}
refers to the Riemann Normal Coordinates centered at the point
\bar{v}
.
M(x_{\bar{v}}(\bar{v}))
refers to
the evaluation of the density weighted lapse
M
at the point
\bar{v}
in the RNCs
\{x_{\bar{v}}\}
centered at
\bar{v}
.
If
\bar{v}=v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
we shall find it useful to relate the evaluation of the lapse
M
at
v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
in the RNCs centered at
v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
to
its evaluation at
v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
in the RNCs centered at
v
. The evaluations in these two sets of coordinates are related by an appropriate factor
of the determinant of the Jacobian between these two sets of coordinates. From (
2.21
) the determinant of this Jacobian is unity to
O(\epsilon^{2})
.
It follows that the two evaluations, in the notation used above, are related as follows:

M(x_{v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon}))=M(x_{v}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon}))+O(\epsilon^{2}).

(4.49)

\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})_{\lambda_{\bar{v}}}}
is the
\gamma
factor for the spin network
(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})_{\lambda_{\bar{v}}}
which is obtained from

S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}
by the action of the inverse determinant operator at
\bar{v}
which changes the intertwiner at
\bar{v}\in S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}

(see the paragraph after (
2.12
) for a precise definition of this inverse determinant metric operator).
Separating out the vertex contributions to (
4.48
) from
v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
and the remaining vertices of
S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}
we write the right hand side of (
4.48
) as:

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{\bar{v}\in V_{GR}(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})}M(x_{\bar{v}}(\bar{v}))g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})_{\lambda_{\bar{v}}}}(\prod_{\bar{v}^{\prime}\neq\bar{v}}f(\bar{v}^{\prime}))(\sum_{\bar{I}_{\bar{v}}=1}^{N_{\bar{v}}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{\bar{I}_{\bar{v}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(\bar{v}))

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{\bar{v}\neq v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}M(x_{\bar{v}}(\bar{v}))g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})_{\lambda_{\bar{v}}}}(\prod_{\bar{v}^{\prime}\neq\bar{v},v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}f(\bar{v}^{\prime}))f(v_{I_{v},\epsilon})(\sum_{\bar{I}_{\bar{v}}=1}^{N_{\bar{v}}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{\bar{I}_{\bar{v}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(\bar{v}))

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{8\pi}M(x_{v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon}))g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})_{\lambda_{v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}f(v^{\prime}))(\sum_{J_{v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{J_{v_{I,\epsilon}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v_{I,\epsilon})).

(4.50)

In the second line, since
\bar{v}\neq v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
, equation (
3.27
) implies that

\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})_{\lambda_{\bar{v}}}}=\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{\bar{v}}}}.

(4.51)

In the third line, equations (
3.15
) and (
3.23
) imply that:

\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})_{\lambda_{v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}}}=\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}.

(4.52)

Let us now simplify the second line of (
4.50
) as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{\bar{v}\neq v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}M(x_{\bar{v}}(\bar{v}))g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})_{\lambda_{\bar{v}}}}(\prod_{\bar{v}^{\prime}\neq\bar{v},v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}f(\bar{v}^{\prime}))f(v_{I_{v},\epsilon})(\sum_{\bar{I}_{\bar{v}}=1}^{N_{\bar{v}}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{\bar{I}_{\bar{v}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(\bar{v}))

(4.53)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{\bar{v}\neq v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}M(x_{\bar{v}}(\bar{v}))g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{\bar{v}}}}(\prod_{\bar{v}^{\prime}\neq\bar{v},v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}f(\bar{v}^{\prime}))f(v_{I_{v},\epsilon})(\sum_{\bar{I}_{\bar{v}}=1}^{N_{\bar{v}}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{\bar{I}_{\bar{v}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(\bar{v}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v^{\prime}\neq v}M(x_{v^{\prime}}(v^{\prime}))g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v^{\prime}}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\neq v^{\prime}}f(v^{\prime\prime}))(\sum_{I_{v^{\prime}}=1}^{N_{v^{\prime}}}(\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v^{\prime}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v^{\prime}))

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\epsilon\;\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v^{\prime}\neq v}M(x_{v^{\prime}}(v^{\prime}))g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v^{\prime}}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\neq v^{\prime},v}f(v^{\prime\prime}))\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v))(\sum_{I_{v^{\prime}}=1}^{N_{v^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v^{\prime}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v^{\prime}))

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle O(\epsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v^{\prime}\neq v}M(x_{v^{\prime}}(v^{\prime}))\frac{1}{4}g_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v^{\prime}}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\neq v^{\prime}}f(v^{\prime\prime}))(\sum_{I_{v^{\prime}}=1}^{N_{v^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v^{\prime}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v^{\prime}))

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\epsilon\;\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v^{\prime}\neq v}M(x_{v^{\prime}}(v^{\prime}))\frac{1}{4}g_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v^{\prime}}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\neq v^{\prime},v}f(v^{\prime\prime}))(\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v))(\sum_{I_{v^{\prime}}=1}^{N_{v^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v^{\prime}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v^{\prime}))

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle O(\epsilon^{2})

where we have used (
4.51
) to simplify the gamma factor of the first line to obtain the second, (
4.35
) to expand the second line and finally, (
4.1
) to expand the interkink distance function
of the diffeomorphism child
S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}
in terms of that of its parent. In the first two lines of the above set of equations,
\bar{v}
ranges over all nondegenerate vertices of

S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}

other than
v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
. This range is the same as that of all nondegenerate vertices of
S
other than
v
. Accordingly, in the remaining part of (
4.53
), we have renamed
\bar{v}
as
v^{\prime}

with
v^{\prime}
ranging over all vertices in
V_{GR}(S)
other than
v
.
On the other hand, in the first two lines of the above equations

\bar{v}^{\prime}
ranges over the entire vertex set of

S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}
modulo the restrictions under the product sign.
Renaming
\bar{v}^{\prime}
as
v^{\prime\prime}
in the remaining part of (
4.53
) it is easy to see that this range is the same as that of all vertices in
V(S)
modulo the restrictions under the
product sign.

Next, we simplify the third line of (
4.50
). In order to do we adopt the following simplifying edge enumeration for edges at
v_{I_{v},\epsilon}

which assigns the same identifying number to the deformed counterpart of any edge at
v
as that of its undeformed counterpart. Accordingly we shall assume an enumeration of
these edges such that the
J{}_{v_{I,\epsilon}}
th edge
e_{J{}_{v_{I,\epsilon}}}
at
v_{I,\epsilon}
is the deformed counterpart
of the edge
e_{J_{v}=J{}_{v_{I,\epsilon}}}
at
v
(here the deformation is that corresponding to an electric diffeomorphism along the
I_{v}
th edge of
v
).
We have that:

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}M(x_{v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon}))g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})_{\lambda_{v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}f(v^{\prime}))(\sum_{J_{v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{J_{v_{I,\epsilon}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v_{I,\epsilon}))

(4.54)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}(M(x_{v}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon}))+O(\epsilon^{2}))g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}f(v^{\prime}))(\sum_{J_{v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{J_{v_{I,\epsilon}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v_{I,\epsilon}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}(M(x_{v}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon}))+O(\epsilon^{2}))g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}f(v^{\prime}))\frac{1}{4}(N_{v}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v_{I,\epsilon}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v_{I,\epsilon}))+O(\epsilon^{2}))\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(4.55)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}f(v^{\prime}))\frac{1}{4}N_{v}M(x_{v}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon})){\hat{e}}_{I_{v_{I,\epsilon}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v_{I,\epsilon}))\;\;+\;\;\;\;\;O(\epsilon^{2})

(4.56)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}\frac{1}{4}g_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}f(v^{\prime}))\frac{1}{4}N_{v}M(x_{v}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon})){\hat{e}}_{I_{v_{I,\epsilon}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v_{I,\epsilon}))\;\;+\;\;\;\;\;O(\epsilon^{2}).

(4.57)

where
v^{\prime}
ranges over the entire vertex set of
S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}
other than
v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
, this range coinciding with the entire vertex set of
S
other than
v
.

In (
4.54
) we have used (
4.52
) to simplify the
\gamma
factor. We have also used (
4.49
) in (
4.54
) to transit from the
\{x_{v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}\}
RNCs centered at
v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
to the

\{x_{v}\}
RNCs centered at
v
in the evaluation of the lapse
M
. Since the parameter
\epsilon
is measured with respect to the
\{x_{v}\}
RNCs, this will enable us, subsequently, to expand the lapse
via a Taylor series expansion of the type (
4.35
).

In (
4.55
) we use the ‘upward conical stiffening’ of the edge tangent structure at
v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
as encoded in (
2.26
) to simplify the sum over edge tangents.
In (
4.56
) we use the boundedness of the various pieces of (
4.55
) to separate out the
O(\epsilon^{2})
piece. We shall continue to do this as we go along without explicit mention.
In (
4.57
) we use the contraction behaviour (
4.1
) of the interkink distance function
g
.

Next, we expand the quantity
M(x_{v}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon})){\hat{e}}_{I_{v_{I,\epsilon}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v_{I,\epsilon})
(
4.57
) in a Taylor series about
v
using (
4.29
)-(
4.34
).
Recall that the RNCs are
C^{r-1}
coordinates, the metric
h
is
C^{r-1}
tensor,
f
is a
C^{r}
function and
e_{I_{v}}
is a semianalytic
C^{r}
edge with,
as in (
4.29
)-(
4.34
), semianalytic parameterization
t

such that
v_{I,\epsilon}
is located on the edge
e_{I_{v}}
at parameter value
t=t_{\epsilon}={\bf O}(\epsilon)
and
v
at
t=0
. From (
2.26
) and the numbering convention of edges discussed above, it follows that

{\hat{e}}_{I_{v_{I,\epsilon}}}^{a}
in (
4.57
) is the unit (with respect to the metric
h
at
v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
) edge tangent to the edge
e_{I_{v}}
at the point
v_{I_{v},\epsilon}
located at parameter value
t_{\epsilon}
.

It is convenient to use the following notation. Denote the point on the edge
e_{I_{v}}
at parameter
t
by
e_{I_{v}}(t)
,
and the edge tangent at the point
e_{I_{v}}(t)
by
{{{\dot{e}}^{a}}}_{I_{v}}(t)
. Then the unit (with respect to
h
) edge tangent at this point is:

{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}^{a}}(t)=\frac{{{{\dot{e}}^{a}}}_{I_{v}}(t)}{|{\vec{{\dot{e}}}}_{I_{v}}(t)|}

(4.58)

where
|{\vec{{\dot{e}}}}_{I_{v}}(t)|
is the metric norm of
{{{\dot{e}}^{a}}}_{I_{v}}(t)
i.e.

|{\vec{{\dot{e}}}}_{I_{v}}(t)|^{2}:=h_{ab}(e_{I_{v}}(t)){{{\dot{e}}^{a}}}_{I_{v}}(t){{{\dot{e}}^{b}}}_{I_{v}}(t).

(4.59)

Finally denote
M(x_{v}(e_{I_{v}}(t)))
by
M(t)
.

It then follows that

M(x_{v}(p))({\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}(p)\partial_{a}f(p))|_{p=e_{I_{v}}(t)}=(M(t)\frac{1}{|{\vec{{\dot{e}}}}_{I_{v}}(t)|}\frac{df}{dt})
is a
C^{r-2}
function of
t

and admits a Taylor expansion about
t=0
:

\displaystyle M(x_{v}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon})){\hat{e}}_{I_{v_{I,\epsilon}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v_{I,\epsilon})=(M(t)\frac{1}{|{\vec{{\dot{e}}}}_{I_{v}}(t)|}\frac{df}{dt})|_{t=t\epsilon}

(4.60)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M(t=0)\frac{df}{dt}|_{t=0}+t_{\epsilon}(\frac{d}{dt}(M(t)\frac{1}{|{\vec{{\dot{e}}}}_{I_{v}}(t)|}\frac{df}{dt}))|_{t=0}+O(t_{\epsilon}^{2})

(4.61)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M(x_{v}(v))({\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v))\;+\;\frac{\epsilon}{|{\vec{{\dot{e}}}}_{I_{v}}(t=0)|}(\frac{d}{dt}(M(t)\frac{1}{|{\vec{{\dot{e}}}}_{I_{v}}(t)|}\frac{df}{dt}))|_{t=0}+O(\epsilon^{2})

(4.62)

where in the last line we have used
e_{I_{v}}(t=0)=v
in the first term and (
4.58
) together with (
4.34
).
Finally using the obvious notation:

{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v}}\partial_{a}F(p)|_{p=e_{I_{v}}(t)}\equiv\frac{1}{|{\vec{{\dot{e}}}}_{I_{v}}(t)|}{{{\dot{e}}^{a}}}_{I_{v}}(t)\partial_{a}F(e_{I_{v}}(t))=\frac{1}{|{\vec{{\dot{e}}}}_{I_{v}}(t)|}\frac{dF(e_{I_{v}}(t))}{dt}

(4.63)

where
F=F(e_{I_{v}}(t))
is any differentiable function
F:e_{I_{v}}\rightarrow{\bf C}
,
we can use the Taylor expansion (
4.62
) in (
4.57
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}M(x_{v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}(v_{I_{v},\epsilon}))g_{S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})_{\lambda_{v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}f(v^{\prime}))(\sum_{J_{v_{I_{v},\epsilon}}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{J_{v_{I,\epsilon}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v_{I,\epsilon}))

(4.64)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}\frac{1}{4}g_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v}f(v^{\prime}))\frac{1}{4}N_{v}

\displaystyle\big(M(x_{v}(v)){\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v))+\epsilon{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{b}\partial_{b}(M(x_{v}(v)){\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v)))\big)\;\;+\;\;\;\;\;O(\epsilon^{2}).

Using (
4.53
) and (
4.64
) in (
4.50
) and recalling that (
4.50
) is the right hand side of (
4.48
), it follows that equation (
4.48
) can be written as:

\displaystyle\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})

(4.65)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v^{\prime}\neq v}M(x_{v^{\prime}}(v^{\prime}))\frac{1}{4}g_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v^{\prime}}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\neq v^{\prime}}f(v^{\prime\prime}))(\sum_{I_{v^{\prime}}=1}^{N_{v^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v^{\prime}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v^{\prime}))

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}\frac{1}{4}g_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v}f(v^{\prime}))\frac{1}{4}N_{v}M(x_{v}(v)){\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\epsilon\;\big(\;\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v^{\prime}\neq v}M(x_{v^{\prime}}(v^{\prime}))\frac{1}{4}g_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v^{\prime}}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\neq v^{\prime},v}f(v^{\prime\prime}))(\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v))(\sum_{I_{v^{\prime}}=1}^{N_{v^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v^{\prime}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v^{\prime}))

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}\frac{1}{4}g_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v}f(v^{\prime}))\frac{1}{4}N_{v}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{b}\partial_{b}(M(x_{v}(v)){\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v))\big)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle O(\epsilon^{2})

Note that the
0
th order and
1
st order contributions in
\epsilon
to (
4.65
) are independent of the
\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}
label of the first line of (
4.65
). Hence in the expression in square brackets in
(
4.47
) the combinatorial factor in the denominator cancels with the factor obtained by summing over
\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}
and up to terms of
O(\epsilon^{2})
we have that:

\frac{1}{{}^{N_{v}-1}\!P_{3}}\sum_{\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}}\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})=\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})

(4.66)

with the right hand side of (
4.66
) being given by that of (
4.65
). In (
4.47
) there is also an additional sum over
I_{v}
in the square brackets for this amplitude.
Performing this sum, we obtain the zeroth order in
\epsilon
contribution to the square bracketed term to be

\displaystyle[\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{1}{{}^{N_{v}-1}\!P_{3}}\sum_{\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}}\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})]|_{0}

(4.67)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{v}\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v^{\prime}\neq v}M(x_{v^{\prime}}(v^{\prime}))\frac{1}{4}g_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v^{\prime}}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\neq v^{\prime}}f(v^{\prime\prime}))(\sum_{I_{v^{\prime}}=1}^{N_{v^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v^{\prime}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v^{\prime}))

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle N_{v}\frac{3}{8\pi}\frac{1}{4}g_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v}f(v^{\prime}))M(x_{v}(v))\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v).

On the other hand, using (
4.39
) we have that:

\displaystyle\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v}})

(4.68)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v^{\prime}}M(x_{v^{\prime}}(v^{\prime}))g_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v^{\prime}}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\neq v^{\prime}}f(v^{\prime\prime}))(\sum_{I_{v^{\prime}}=1}^{N_{v^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v^{\prime}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v^{\prime})).

Separating out the contributions from
v
and
v^{\prime}\neq v
in (
4.68
) it is immediate to see from (
4.67
) that:

[\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{1}{{}^{N_{v}-1}\!P_{3}}\sum_{\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}}\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})]|_{0}=N_{v}\frac{1}{4}\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v}}).

(4.69)

This implies that in the numerator of the term inside the limit operation in (
4.47
), the zeroth order in
\epsilon
contribution to the square brackets cancels with the term following it.
Since the denominator contains a factor of
\epsilon
,
O(\epsilon^{2})
contributions to the square bracket vanish in the
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
limit and the only nontrivial contributions come from a sum over
I_{v}

of
{\bf O}(\epsilon)
contributions to the right hand side of (
4.66
). These contributions can be read off from (
4.65
). Putting all this together, we obtain:

\displaystyle\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}(M){\hat{H}}(N)S):=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}(\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M){\hat{H}}_{\epsilon}(N)S))

(4.70)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8\pi}\sum_{v\in V_{GR}(S)}N(x_{v}(v))

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\left(\frac{[\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{1}{{}^{N_{v}-1}\!P_{3}}\sum_{\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}}\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v},I_{v},\vec{\hat{J}}_{v}\epsilon})]-N_{v}{\frac{1}{4}}\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\rightarrow 0}\Psi_{B,f,h}({\hat{H}}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)S_{\lambda_{v}})}{\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{3}{8\pi})^{2}\sum_{v\in V_{GR}(S)}N(x_{v}(v))\frac{1}{4}g_{S}

\displaystyle\Big(\;\;\sum_{v^{\prime}\neq v}M(x_{v^{\prime}}(v^{\prime}))\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v^{\prime}}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\neq v^{\prime},v}f(v^{\prime\prime}))(\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v))(\sum_{I_{v^{\prime}}=1}^{N_{v^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{4}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v^{\prime}}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v^{\prime}))

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\neq v}f(v^{\prime}))\frac{1}{4}N_{v}\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}{\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{b}\partial_{b}(M(x_{v}(v)){\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v))\;\;\Big).

Here we have denoted
N(x(v))
(see (
4.47
)) by
N(x_{v}(v))
to bring the notation in line with that used in the analysis of (
4.50
). Also
we have replaced
g_{S_{\lambda_{v}}}
by
g_{S}
because
S_{\lambda_{v}}
and
S
only differ by their intertwiners at
v
and the interkink distance function is insensitive
to this difference as it only depends on the kink structure of the state. For the equations from (
4.57
) on, we have not explicitly mentioned the ranges of vertex labels
under the summation and product signs as these are straightforward to infer as we go along so that in the final equation (
4.70
) above these ranges are as follows.
The vertex label
v
as indicated ranges over the set
V_{GR}(S)
of all nondegenerate (and necessarily GR, since
S
has overlap with
B
) vertices of
S
.
The vertex label
v^{\prime}
also ranges over
V_{GR}(S)
whereas the vertex label
v^{\prime\prime}
ranges over the set
V(S)
of all (necessarily GR but not necessarily nondegenerate)
vertices of
S
.

4.3.3
Constraint Commutator

The continuum limit commutator amplitude can be read off from the part of the continuum limit product amplitude which is antisymmetric under interchange of the lapse functions
M,N

(see (
4.41
), (
4.42
)).
It follows from the considerations of sections
4.3.1
-
4.3.3
that the continuum limit commutator amplitude is only non-trivial for spin networks states which have overlap in
B
,
in which case this amplitude commutator can be read off from the part of (
4.70
).

Our final result is then

\displaystyle\Psi_{B,f,h}([{\hat{H}}(M),{\hat{H}}(N)]S)

(4.71)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;{\rm if}\;S\;{\rm has}\;{\rm no}\;{\rm overlap}\;{\rm in}\;B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{3}{8\pi})^{2}\frac{1}{16}g_{S}\sum_{v\in V_{GR}(S)}\gamma_{(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}N_{v}(\prod_{v^{\prime}\in V(S),v^{\prime}\neq v}f(v^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\Big(\;\;N(x_{v}(v)){\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{b}\partial_{b}M(x_{v}(v))-M(x_{v}(v)){\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{b}\partial_{b}N(x_{v}(v))\;\;\Big){\hat{e}}_{I_{v}}^{a}\partial_{a}f(v)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;{\rm if}\;S\;{\rm has}\;{\rm overlap}\;{\rm in}\;B\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(4.72)

4.4
On the importance of embeddable abstract spin networks

Consider the off shell state
\Psi_{B,f,h}
. Let us fix the Bra Set
B
.
Note that the calculations and results of sections
4.2
,
4.3
go through for fixed
B
and
any
choice of
f,h
. In particular, even if we change
h
,
the calculations hold despite the fact that we are changing the regulating RNCs. The reason for this is tied to the role of embeddable abstract spin networks in the definition of
B
in section
3
.
Specifically, the associated decorated abstract spin network (see section
3.1
) for each deformed child generated by the action of the constraint on a parent is
independent
of the metric
h
used to define the deformation.
This implies that if a child generated by the constraint action with respect to RNCs defined by a metric
h
has overlap with
B
, the corresponding child generated with respect to RNCs defined with respect to a different metric
h^{\prime}

will also have overlap with
B
, this overlap only depending on the (same) underlying decorated abstract spin network associated with the
h
-child and the
h^{\prime}
-child.
It is important to emphasize that while we can, using the techniques of Appendix
D
, relate constraint actions at different
\epsilon
with respect to the
same
metric
h
by diffeomorphisms if we so wish,
we do not know if constraint actions defined with respect to different metrics are diffeomorphic. Since the metrics under consideration are all diffeomorphic to each other, it may be possible to define these actions
so that they are indeed diffeomorphic to each other.
Instead,
by going beyond diffeomorphism classes to ‘decorated abstract spin network’-classes
in the definition of Bra Sets, we bypass this issue. The consequent fact, that the results of sections
4.2
,
4.3
go through for fixed
B
regardless of
h
, is a crucial ingredient in our proof
of diffeomorphism covariance of the constraint action in section
6
. A potential advantage of the use of abstract structures over a putative attempt to incorporate the desired property of the constraint
actions for different
h
being diffeomorphic, is that while the latter is expected to leave an imprint of the diffemorphism class of metrics
{\cal H}_{h_{0}}
on the off-shell space, the former could possibly lead to an
off shell space independent of the choice of (diffeomorphism class of)
h_{0}
. In a similar fashion an insistence on abstract structures may conceivably also lead to a manifestly
h_{0}
independent
physical state space; this would be desireable as it would signal independence of physics from the choice of regulating structure.

5
Quantization of
\{H(M),H(N)\}

For notational convenience denote the Poisson bracket
\{H(M),H(N)\}
by
O(M,N)
.
From (
2.9
), we have that on the Gauss Law constraint surface:

\displaystyle\{H[M],H[N]\}:=O(M,N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}^{3}xF_{ab}^{k}E_{k}^{b}\omega_{c}q^{-2/3}E_{i}^{a}E_{i}^{c},

(5.1)

\displaystyle\omega_{c}=\left(N\partial_{c}M-M\partial_{c}N\right)

(5.2)

The expression on the right hand side is the diffeomorphism constraint smeared with a metric dependent ‘shift’ vector field:

L^{a}=\omega_{c}q^{-2/3}E_{i}^{a}E_{i}^{c}.

(5.3)

We shall construct the operator
{\hat{O}}(M,N)
corresponding to
\{H(M),H(N)\}
by regulating the right hand side of (
5.1
) in three steps. First, in section
5.1
we construct a regulated
operator
{\hat{L}}^{a}_{\epsilon}
corresponding to the ‘metric shift’
L^{a}
. Next, in section
5.2
we use this regulated version to construct a regulated version
{\hat{O}}_{\epsilon}(M,N)
of
{\hat{O}}(M,N)

as a
\frac{1}{\epsilon}
times the difference between a finite ‘
\epsilon
size’ diffeomorphism and the identity. Importantly, in line with the classical expression, this diffeomorphism
will be an ordinary
semianalytic
diffeomorphism rather than an electric diffeomorphism.
Finally in section
5.3
we compute the continuum limit action of
{\hat{O}}_{\epsilon}(M,N)
on the basis state
\Psi_{B,f,h}
and show that this continuum limit agrees with the
constraint commutator continuum limit (
4.72
).

5.1
Construction of
{\hat{L}}^{a}_{\epsilon}

Denote the metric shift at point
p
by
L^{a}(p)
.
In this section we construct the corresponding regulated metric shift operator
{\hat{L}}^{a}_{\epsilon}(p)
. Our final result is
as follows.
For every nondegenerate vertex
v
of
S
, consider a small neighborhood
B_{2\epsilon}(v)
of coordinate size
\epsilon
as measured by the RNC’s at
v
.
Let
\epsilon
be small enough that
B_{2\epsilon}(v)
contains no other vertex of
S
. Then
{\hat{L}}^{a}_{\epsilon}(p)
is non-trivial only if
p\in B_{2\epsilon}(v)
for some
non-degenerate vertex
v
of
S
with action

{\hat{L}}^{a}_{\epsilon}(p)S=\frac{2}{(\frac{4\pi}{3})^{2}}\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}(j_{I})(j_{I}+1)(\omega_{c}(v){\hat{e}}^{c}_{I}(v)){\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}(p)(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}

(5.4)

where
{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}(p)
is a regulated version of the unit coordinate edge tangent
{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v}}(v)
supported only in

B_{2\epsilon}(v)
and defined through equations (
D.10
), (
D.11
) with the identification
f^{\mu}\equiv{\hat{e}}^{\mu}_{I_{v},v}
in the coordinate system defined in section
D.2
.
It follows from this definition that as in the regularization of the electric shift
[
2
]
, for every small enough
\epsilon
,
{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}(p)
is chosen such that on the intersection of
the
I_{v}
th edge
e_{I_{v}}
with
B_{2\epsilon}(v)
,
{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}(p)
is parallel to the edge tangent to
e_{I_{v}}
i.e.

{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}(p)=\lambda(p){\dot{e}}^{a}_{I_{v}}(p)\;{\rm for}\;p\in e_{I_{v}}\cap B_{2\epsilon}(v),

(5.5)

and that, as in
[
2
]
,
\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}(p)
vanishes everywhere except at
v
where it is equal to
{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v}}(v)
.
It is in this sense that
{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}(p)
is a regularization of
{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v}}(v)
.

The rest of this section is devoted to a derivation of (
5.4
).

Fix RNC’s at a point
q\in\Sigma
. Let
B_{\epsilon}(q)
denote an open coordinate ball of radius
\epsilon
centered at
q
. Let
{\bar{B}}_{\epsilon^{n}}(q)
be the closure of
the open ball of radius
\epsilon^{n},\;n>>1
centered at
q
. Define
B^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(q)
as the exterior of
{\bar{B}}_{\epsilon^{n}}(q)
in
B_{\epsilon}(q)
:

B^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(q)=B_{\epsilon}(q)-{\bar{B}}_{\epsilon^{n}}(q).

(5.6)

In what follows we shall often replace the argument consisting of the point
q
by its coordinate value i.e. we write
B^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)

and we implicitly mean
B^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(q)
for
q
with coordinates
x^{\mu},\mu=1,2,3
. We shall also often employ a similar notation for arguments of fields so that, for e.g.

E^{a}_{i}(x)
will mean the evaluation of
E^{a}_{i}(q)
at a point
q
which has coordinates
x^{\mu},\mu=1,2,3
with density weights also evaluated with respect to
\{x\}
.

Next, recall from
[
2
]
and the discussion in section
3.4.3
, we may regulate
{\hat{q}}^{-\frac{1}{3}}(p)

to have non-trivial action only if
p
coincides with a nondegenerate vertex
v
of
S
in which case we have for all small enough
\delta
:

({\hat{q}}_{\delta}^{-\frac{1}{3}}(v))S:=\delta^{2}S_{\lambda_{v}}.

(5.7)

Next, we define the classical approximant
L^{a}_{\tau,\eta}(p)\;\eta<<\tau
:

L^{a}_{\tau,\eta}(p):=\omega_{c}(p)\left(\frac{1}{{\frac{4}{3}}\pi\tau^{3}}\int_{B_{\tau}^{\prime}(p)}d^{3}x\;(\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(x)}}d^{3}\bar{x}\frac{E^{c}_{i}(\bar{x})}{\frac{4}{3}\pi\eta^{3}})E^{a}_{i}(x)\right)({q_{\tau}^{-\frac{1}{3}}}(p))({q_{\eta}^{-\frac{1}{3}}}(p))

(5.8)

where
({q_{\delta}^{-\frac{1}{3}}}(p))
is the classical precursor of (
5.7
)
defined through an appropriate power of the volume of
B_{\delta}(v)
measured with respect to the metric
q_{ab}
as in Reference
[
2
]
and Footnote
4
.

It is straightforward to check that:

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow 0}(\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0}L^{a}_{\tau,\eta}(p))=L^{a}(p)

(5.9)

so that
L^{a}_{\tau,\eta}(p)\;\eta<<\tau
is indeed an approximant to the metric shift. Next, we construct a quantization of this approximant. We choose an operator ordering in which
quantum operators appear in the same order in which their classical correspondents appear in (
5.8
) so that
{\hat{q}_{\eta}^{-\frac{1}{3}}}(p)
appears right most.
Then if
p
is not a nondegenerate vertex of
S
, we have, for all small enough
\eta
, that

{\hat{L}}^{a}_{\tau,\eta}(p)S=0.

(5.10)

If
p=v
is a nondegenerate vertex of
S
, we have that:

\displaystyle{\hat{L}}^{a}_{\tau,\eta}(v)S

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\omega_{c}(v)\left(\frac{1}{{\frac{4}{3}}\pi\tau^{3}}\int_{B_{\tau}^{\prime}(v)}d^{3}x\;(\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(x)}}d^{3}\bar{x}\frac{{\hat{E}}^{c}_{i}(\bar{x})}{\frac{4}{3}\pi\eta^{3}}){\hat{E}}^{a}_{i}(x)\right)({{\hat{q}}_{\tau}^{-\frac{1}{3}}}(v))({{\hat{q}}_{\eta}^{-\frac{1}{3}}}(v))S

(5.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{{\frac{4}{3}}\pi})^{2}\frac{1}{\tau\eta}\omega_{c}(v)\int_{B_{\tau}^{\prime}(v)}d^{3}x\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(x)}}d^{3}\bar{x}{\hat{E}}^{c}_{i}(\bar{x})\;\;{\hat{E}}^{a}_{i}(x)(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}

(5.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{{\frac{4}{3}}\pi})^{2}\frac{1}{\tau\eta}\omega_{c}(v)\int_{B_{\tau}^{\prime}(v)}d^{3}x\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(x)}}d^{3}\bar{x}{\hat{E}}^{c}_{i}(\bar{x})

\displaystyle i\sum_{I}\int_{e_{I}}dt_{I}{\dot{e}}^{a}_{I}\delta^{3}(x,e_{I}(t_{I}))(h_{e_{I}}(1,t_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}}

(5.13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{{\frac{4}{3}}\pi})^{2}\frac{i}{\tau\eta}\omega_{c}(v)\sum_{I}\int_{\tau_{n,I}}^{\tau_{I}}dt_{I}{\dot{e}}^{a}_{I}\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(e_{I}(t_{I}))}}d^{3}\bar{x}{\hat{E}}^{c}_{i}(\bar{x})(h_{e_{I}}(1,t_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}}.\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(5.14)

Above, we have used (
5.7
) in (
5.12
). The standard action of the triad operator in (
5.12
) yields (
5.13
). In (
5.13
), similar to our considerations in
[
2
]
,
\tau_{i}
is the matrix representative
of the
i
th generator of

su(2)
in the
j_{I}
spin representation which colors the edge
e_{I}
in
S
. For notational convenience we have suppressed the subscript
v
in the edge index
I
and edge parameter
t_{I}
.
Integration over the Dirac delta function in (
5.13
) yields (
5.14
). For small enough
\tau
, this leads to an integration over the part of the edge
e_{I}
between the parameter values

\tau_{n,I}
and
\tau_{I}
with
e(\tau_{n,I})
being the point of intersection of
e_{I}
with the boundary of
{\bar{B}}_{\tau^{n}}(v)
and
e(\tau_{I})
the point of intersection of
e_{I}
with the boundary
of
{\bar{B}}_{{\tau}(v)}
.

In what follows we retain only leading order
contributions in the small parameters
\eta,\tau
to (
5.14
). We follow the standard practice employed in LQG derivations of operator actions, of estimating these contributions by assuming that the
spin network wave function
S\equiv S(A)
is evaluated on smooth (i.e.
C^{r}
) connections rather than distributional elements of the quantum configuration space. We proceed as follows.

Next, we evaluate the action of
\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(e_{I}(t_{I}))}}d^{3}\bar{x}{\hat{E}}^{c}_{i}(\bar{x})
in (
5.14
) on the holonomy combination to its right.
Note that
S
(and hence
(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}
)
is a monomial in each of its constituent edge
holonomies and for small enough
\tau,\eta
,
{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(e_{I}(t_{I}))}}
intersects
S
only in a subset of its
I
th edge. Note also
the argument
\bar{x}
of the triad operator is not coincident with
e_{I}({t_{I}})
in this subset. It follows that:

\displaystyle\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(e_{I}(t_{I}))}}d^{3}\bar{x}{\hat{E}}^{c}_{i}(\bar{x})(h_{e_{I}}(1,t_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}}

(5.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(e_{I}(t_{I}))}}d^{3}\bar{x}\Big(\int_{t_{I}}^{1}d\bar{t}_{I}{\dot{e}}^{c}_{I}\delta^{3}(\bar{x},e_{I}(\bar{t}_{I}))(h_{e_{I}}(1,\bar{t}_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(\bar{t}_{I},t_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{I}}d\bar{t}_{I}{\dot{e}}^{c}_{I}\delta^{3}(\bar{x},e_{I}(\bar{t}_{I}))(h_{e_{I}}(1,t_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},\bar{t}_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(\bar{t}_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\Big)\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}}.

Next, consider
e(t_{I})
which is a coordinate distance of at least
2\eta
away from
e_{I}(\tau_{n,I})
as well as
e_{I}({\tau_{I}})
.
The parameter range for which this is satisfied is almost all of
\tau_{n,I}<t_{I}<\tau_{I}
except for a parameter range of
O(\eta)
near the endpoints
\tau_{n,I},\tau_{I}
(the fact that the parameter
range is of the same order of the distance between the end points of the parameter range is implied by the
C^{r}
property of the edge
e_{I}
through (
4.34
)).
For such
t_{I}
, the intersection of
e_{I}
with
{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(e_{I}(t_{I}))}}

is the union of two disjoint segments of
e_{I}
, one
between the parameter values
t_{I}+\eta^{+}_{n,I},t_{I}+\eta_{I}^{+}
and one between the parameter values
t_{I}-\eta_{I}^{-},t_{I}-\eta^{-}_{n,I}

where
\eta_{\pm},\eta_{n,I}^{\pm}
are parameter values such that:

(i)
e_{I}
intersects the boundary of
{\bar{B}}_{\eta^{n}}(e_{I}(t_{I}))
in the pair of points
e_{I}(t_{I}+\eta^{+}_{n,I}),e_{I}(t_{I}-\eta^{+}_{n,I})
.

(ii)
e_{I}
intersects the boundary of
{\bar{B}}_{\eta}(e_{I}(t_{I}))
in the pair of points
e_{I}(t_{I}+\eta^{+}_{I}),e_{I}(t_{I}-\eta^{+}_{I})
.

For such
e_{I}(t_{I})
we continue on from (
5.15
). Integrating the delta function over
d^{3}\bar{x}
and using (i), (ii) above we get:

\displaystyle\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(e_{I}(t_{I}))}}d^{3}\bar{x}{\hat{E}}^{c}_{i}(\bar{x})(h_{e_{I}}(1,t_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}}

(5.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\Big(\int_{t_{I}+\eta^{+}_{n,I}}^{t_{I}+\eta_{I}^{+}}d\bar{t}_{I}{\dot{e}}^{c}_{I}(h_{e_{I}}(1,\bar{t}_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(\bar{t}_{I},t_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{t_{I}-\eta_{I}^{-}}^{t_{I}-\eta^{-}_{n,I}}d\bar{t}_{I}{\dot{e}}^{c}_{I}(h_{e_{I}}(1,t_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},\bar{t}_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(\bar{t}_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\Big)\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\Big(\int_{t_{I}+\eta^{+}_{n,I}}^{t_{I}+\eta_{I}^{+}}d\bar{t}_{I}{\dot{e}}^{c}_{I}(h_{e_{I}}(1,t_{I})\tau_{i}\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{t_{I}-\eta_{I}^{-}}^{t_{I}-\eta^{-}_{n,I}}d\bar{t}_{I}{\dot{e}}^{c}_{I}(h_{e_{I}}(1,t_{I})\tau_{i}\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\Big)\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}}\;\;\;+\;O(\eta^{2})

(5.17)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i(j_{I})(j_{I}+1)\Big(\int_{t_{I}+\eta^{+}_{n,I}}^{t_{I}+\eta_{I}^{+}}d\bar{t}_{I}{\dot{e}}^{c}_{I}+\int_{t_{I}-\eta_{I}^{-}}^{t_{I}-\eta^{-}_{n,I}}d\bar{t}_{I}{\dot{e}}^{c}_{I}\Big)(h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}})+O(\eta^{2})\;

(5.18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i(j_{I})(j_{I}+1)\big((e_{I}^{c}({t_{I}+\eta_{I}^{+}})-e_{I}^{c}(t_{I}+\eta^{+}_{n,I}))+(e^{c}(t_{I}-\eta^{-}_{n,I})-e^{c}(t_{I}-\eta_{I}^{-}))\big)

\displaystyle h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}}\;+\;O(\eta^{2})

(5.19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i(j_{I})(j_{I}+1)(2{\hat{e}}^{c}(t_{I})\eta)(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}\;+O\;(\eta^{2}).

(5.20)

Here, in the first integral of (
5.16
) we have used
h_{e_{I}}(1,\bar{t}_{I})=h_{e_{I}}(1,t_{I})(h_{e_{I}}(\bar{t}_{I},t_{I}))^{-1}
, the fact that parameter and distance orders are the same for
C^{r}
edges (
4.34
) and
the smooth connection expansion of holonomies
h_{e_{I}}(\bar{t}_{I},t_{I})={\bf 1}+O(\eta)
, and similarly for the second integral. This results in (
5.17
) from (
5.16
).
To obtain (
5.18
) we use the standard
su(2)
representation property
\tau_{i}\tau_{i}=-j_{I}(j_{I}+1){\bf 1}
.
The integrations in (
5.18
) yield the differences between the RN coordinates of the
I
th edge at the parameter end points for each of the two integrals therein so as to yield (
5.19
). It is straightforward to use expansions of the type
(
4.29
) together with
n>>1
and (i)-(ii) above to show that the contribution of these coordinate differences in (
5.19
) yields, to
O(\eta^{2})
, the
(2{\hat{e}}^{c}(t_{I})\eta)
factor in (
5.20
).
Finally from the fact that
(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}
is a monomial in its edge holonomies, we replace

h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}}
in (
5.19
) by
(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}
in (
5.20
).

Next, let us estimate the contribution to (
5.14
) from those
e(t_{I})
which are within a distance of
2\eta
from
e_{I}(\tau_{n,I})
or
e_{I}({\tau_{I}})
.
Integrating over the delta functions in (
5.15
) yields:

\displaystyle\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(e_{I}(t_{I}))}}d^{3}\bar{x}{\hat{E}}^{c}_{i}(\bar{x})(h_{e_{I}}(1,t_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}}

(5.21)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\Big(\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(e_{I}(t_{I}))}\cap e_{1,t_{I}}}d\bar{t}_{I}{\dot{e}}^{c}_{I}(h_{e_{I}}(1,\bar{t}_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(\bar{t}_{I},t_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle i\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(e_{I}(t_{I}))}\cap e_{0,t_{I}}}d\bar{t}_{I}{\dot{e}}^{c}_{I}(h_{e_{I}}(1,t_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},\bar{t}_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(\bar{t}_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\Big)\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}}

Using the boundedness of the functions of the connection encountered in (
5.21
) together with (
4.29
) we have that:

\displaystyle|\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(e_{I}(t_{I}))}\cap e_{(1,t_{I})}}d\bar{t}_{I}{\dot{e}}^{\mu}_{I}(h_{e_{I}}(1,\bar{t}_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(\bar{t}_{I},t_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}}|

(5.22)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle|\int_{B_{{\eta}^{\prime}(e_{I}(t_{I}))}\cap e_{(0,t_{I})}}d\bar{t}_{I}{\dot{e}}^{\mu}_{I}(h_{e_{I}}(1,t_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(t_{I},\bar{t}_{I})\tau_{i}h_{e_{I}}(\bar{t}_{I},0))^{C}{}_{D}\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}}{\partial h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{C}{}_{D}}|

\displaystyle\leq{\bar{C}}\eta

for some
{\bar{C}}>0

where
{\dot{e}}^{\mu}_{I}
are RNC components of the edge tangent
{\dot{e}}^{a}_{I}
.
Note that this sort of a bound is independent of the parameter value
t_{I}
and hence valid for all
e(t_{I})
whether or not within a distance of
2\eta
from
e_{I}(\tau_{n,I})
or
e_{I}({\tau_{I}})
.
Denote the set of points
e(t_{I})
within a distance of
2\eta
from
e_{I}(\tau_{n,I})
or
e_{I}({\tau_{I}})
by
{\bf U}_{I,2\eta}
and denote the set of parameter values
t_{I}
of these points by

U_{I,2\eta,}

From expansions of the type (
4.29
) it follows that

|\int_{U_{I,2\eta}}dt_{I}{\dot{e}}^{\mu}_{I}|\leq{\bar{C}}_{1}\eta

(5.23)

for some
{\bar{C}}_{1}>0
.
Denote the open interval in parameter space with end points
\tau_{n,I},{\tau_{I}}
by
(\tau_{n,I},{\tau_{I}})
.
We may write (
5.14
) as:

\displaystyle{\hat{L}}^{a}_{\tau,\eta}(v)S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{{\frac{4}{3}}\pi})^{2}\frac{i}{\tau\eta}\omega_{c}(v)\left(\sum_{I}[\int_{(\tau_{n,I},{\tau_{I}})-U_{I,2\eta}}dt_{I}{\dot{e}}^{a}_{I}\big(-i(j_{I})(j_{I}+1)2{\hat{e}}^{c}(t_{I})\eta(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}\big)]+O(\eta^{2})\right)\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(5.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{{\frac{4}{3}}\pi})^{2}\frac{i}{\tau\eta}\omega_{c}(v)\left(\sum_{I}[\int_{(\tau_{n,I},{\tau_{I}})}dt_{I}{\dot{e}}^{a}_{I}\big(-i(j_{I})(j_{I}+1)2{\hat{e}}^{c}(t_{I})\eta(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}\big)]+O(\eta^{2})\right)

(5.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{{\frac{4}{3}}\pi})^{2}\frac{i}{\tau}\omega_{c}(v)\left(\sum_{I}[\int_{(\tau_{n,I},{\tau_{I}})}dt_{I}{\dot{e}}^{a}_{I}\big(-i(j_{I})(j_{I}+1)2{\hat{e}}^{c}(t_{I})(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}]+O(\eta)\right).

(5.26)

In (
5.14
) the
t_{I}
integration range can be divided into
U_{I,2\eta}
and its complement. The integral over
U_{I,2\eta}
may be bounded using the boundedness of the RNC components
\omega_{\mu}(v)
of
\omega_{c}(v)
together with
(
5.22
) and (
5.23
) to obtain the

O(\eta^{2})
term in
(
5.24
). For the remaining range of integration we use (
5.20
) to get the rest of (
5.24
).
In (
5.25
) we increase the range of the complement of
U_{I,2\eta}
back to its original range at the cost of an extra integral over
U_{I,2\eta}
. Using the bound (
5.23
)
it is straightforward to see that this extra integral also yields a term of
O(\eta^{2})
and we obtain (
5.26
).
Using expansions of the type (
4.29
) in (
5.26
) together with
n>>1
yields:

\displaystyle{\hat{L}}^{a}_{\tau,\eta}(v)S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{{\frac{4}{3}}\pi})^{2}\frac{2}{\tau}\omega_{c}(v)\left(\sum_{I}\tau{\hat{e}}^{c}_{I}(t_{I}=0){\hat{e}}^{a}_{I}(t_{I}=0)(j_{I})(j_{I}+1)(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}+O(\tau^{2})+O(\eta)\right)\;\;\;\;\;

(5.27)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left((\frac{2}{{\frac{4}{3}}\pi})^{2}\omega_{c}(v)\sum_{I}{\hat{e}}^{c}_{I}(t_{I}=0){\hat{e}}^{a}_{I}(t_{I}=0)(j_{I})(j_{I}+1)(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}\right)+O(\tau)+\frac{O(\eta)}{\tau}.

(5.28)

Taking the limit
\eta\rightarrow 0
followed by that of
\tau\rightarrow 0
yields

{\hat{L}}^{a}(v)S:=(\frac{2}{{\frac{4}{3}}\pi})^{2}\omega_{c}(v)\sum_{I}{\hat{e}}^{c}_{I}(t_{I}=0){\hat{e}}^{a}_{I}(t_{I}=0)(j_{I})(j_{I}+1)(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}

(5.29)

Finally,
exactly
as for the electric shift
[
2
]
we replace
{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I}(t_{I}=0)
by
{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I,\epsilon}(p)
in (
5.29
) to obtain the desired result:

{\hat{L}}^{a}_{\epsilon}(p)S:=(\frac{2}{{\frac{4}{3}}\pi})^{2}\omega_{c}(v)\sum_{I}{\hat{e}}^{c}_{I}(t_{I}=0){\hat{e}}^{a}_{I,\epsilon}(p)(j_{I})(j_{I}+1)(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}.

(5.30)

Since
{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I,\epsilon}(p)
is a
C^{r-1}
vector field of compact support around
v
such that
\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I,\epsilon}(p)
is nonvanishing only when
p=v
with

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I,\epsilon}(v)={\hat{e}}^{a}_{I}(t_{I}=0)(v)
, it follows that
\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}{\hat{L}}^{a}_{\epsilon}(p)S
is also non-trivial only at
v
where it
equals the right hand side of (
5.29
). Hence, from (
5.10
) and (
5.29
) it follows that (
5.30
) defines a regulation of the quantum metric shift operator

{\hat{L}}^{a}
.

5.2
Construction of
{\hat{O}}_{\epsilon}(M,N)

From (
5.1
) we define the operator correspondent of
{O}(M,N)
at regularization parameter
\epsilon
to be:

{\hat{O}_{\epsilon}(M,N)}S=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}x{\hat{F}}_{ab}^{k}{\hat{E}}_{k}^{b}{\hat{L}}^{a}_{\epsilon}S

(5.31)

with
{\hat{L}}^{a}_{\epsilon}S
given by (
5.4
). Substituting from (
5.4
) we obtain:

{\hat{O}_{\epsilon}(M,N)}S=\frac{2}{(\frac{4\pi}{3})^{2}}\sum_{v}(\omega_{c}(v){\hat{e}}^{c}_{I}(v))\sum_{I_{v}=1}^{N_{v}}(j_{I})(j_{I}+1)\int_{B_{2\epsilon}(v)}\mathrm{d}^{3}x{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}{\hat{F}}_{ab}^{k}{\hat{E}}_{k}^{b}(S_{\lambda_{v}})_{\lambda_{v}}.

(5.32)

where the sum is over all nondegnerate vertices
v
in
S
.
The operator under the integral is exactly the diffeomorphism constraint smeared with the
C^{r}
vector field
{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}
taking on the role of a shift vector field.
From
[
23
]
, we expect that the diffeomorphism constraint operator smeared with this shift can be expressed
in terms of the difference between a small diffeomorphism and
the identity divided by the smallness parameter.
In section
5.2.1
we
show that this expectation is borne out when
v
is a GR vertex, the small parameter being
\epsilon
.
In order to do so we use heuristic argumentation which closely parallels that underlying the derivation of the Hamiltonian constraint action in
[
2
]
, the
only significant difference with
[
2
]
being that the finite transformation generated by
{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}
is taken to be a semianalytic diffeomorphism rather than an electric diffeomorphism.
In section
5.2.2
we consider the case that
v
is an NGR vertex. In contrast to our treatment of the GR case, here we provide a heuristic argumentation which does not involve the area of the loops
formed by segments of the deformed and undeformed edges, but which directly seeks to write the operator under the integral as the difference between a small diffeomorphism and the identity.

The reason we adopt different approaches for the GR and NGR cases is as follows. Recall that in the case of the electric diffeomorphisms which underlie the Hamiltonian constraint action,
we transited from (
2.13
) to (
2.14
) in section
2.1.2
so as to conveniently absorb overall factors by changing the area of the small loops
l_{IJ,\epsilon}
, these small loops
being tied to the choice of electric diffeomorphism
[
2
]
.
It turns out to be crucial, for the satisfaction of the anomaly free condition, to absorb overall factors in a similar manner for the action (
5.32
) for the GR case. Clearly, in order to do so, we require an argumentation in which
small loops of area
O(\epsilon^{2})
play a key role.
This is the reason we
rely on the argumentation of
[
2
]
for the GR case. In contrast, when
v
is NGR, the anomaly free condition does not require such an absorption of overall factors and
a more direct heuristic argumentation suffices for our purposes.

5.2.1
The case when
v
is GR

Due to the close similarity with the considerations of
[
2
]
we shall be succinct, for details the reader is urged to consult Reference
[
2
]
.

Let us focus on the contribution from a nondegenerate vertex
v
of
S
. To avoid notational clutter we suppress the subscript
v
at various places.
We have:

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2\epsilon}(v)}\mathrm{d}^{3}x{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}{\hat{F}}_{ab}^{k}{\hat{E}}_{k}^{b}(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}

(5.33)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\sum_{J}\int_{B_{2\epsilon}(v)}\mathrm{d}^{3}x{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I,\epsilon}{\hat{F}}_{ab}^{k}\int_{e_{J}}dt_{J}{\dot{e}}^{a}(t_{J})\delta^{3}(x,e_{J}(t_{J}))[h_{e_{J}}(1,t_{J})\tau_{k}h_{e_{J}}(t_{J},0)]^{A_{J}}_{\;\;B_{J}}\frac{\partial{(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}}}{\partial h_{e_{J}\;B_{J}}^{A_{J}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\sum_{J}\int_{0}^{t_{\epsilon,J,I}}dt_{J}({\hat{e}}^{a}_{I,\epsilon}{\hat{F}}_{ab}^{k}){\dot{e}}^{b}_{J}(t_{J})[h_{e_{J}}(1,t_{J})\tau_{k}h_{e_{J}}(t_{J},0)]^{A_{J}}_{\;\;B_{J}}\frac{\partial{(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}}}{\partial h_{e_{J}\;B_{J}}^{A_{J}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\sum_{J}\int_{0}^{t_{\epsilon,J,I}}dt_{J}({\hat{e}}^{a}_{I,\epsilon}{\hat{F}}_{ab}^{k}){\dot{e}}^{b}_{J}(t_{J})h_{e_{J}}(1,0)({\tau_{k}}+O(\epsilon))^{A_{J}}_{\;\;B_{J}}\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}}{\partial h_{e_{J}\;B_{J}}^{A_{J}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\sum_{J\neq I}\int_{0}^{t_{\epsilon,J,I}}dt_{J}({\hat{e}}^{a}_{I,\epsilon}{F}_{ab}^{k}){\dot{e}}^{b}_{J}(t_{J})(h_{e_{J}}(1,0)\tau_{k})^{A_{J}}_{\;\;B_{J}}\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}}{\partial h_{e_{J}\;B_{J}}^{A_{J}}}.

In the second line above we used the standard action of the triad operator. The third line results from integrating the delta function in the second over
d^{3}x
with the edge
e_{J}
intersecting the
boundary of
B_{2\epsilon}(v)
in a single point with parameter value
t_{\epsilon,J,I}
. In the 4th line we use the standard holonomy expansion for smooth connections in conjunction with expansions of type (
4.29
).
In the 5th line we retain only the leading order term from the 4th line and use (
5.5
) to restrict the sum over
J
.

From an analysis identical to
[
2
]
we have that:

\int_{B_{2\epsilon}(v)}\int_{0}^{t_{\epsilon,J,I}}dt_{J}{\dot{e}}^{b}_{J}{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I,\epsilon}{F}_{ab}^{i}\tau_{i}=-\frac{(h_{{\bar{l}}_{IJ,\epsilon}}-{\bf 1})}{\epsilon}+O(\epsilon^{2})

(5.34)

where the loop
l_{IJ,\epsilon}
is defined as:

\bar{l}_{IJ,\epsilon}=({e_{J}}_{(t_{\epsilon,J,I},0)})^{-1}\circ{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}({e_{J}}_{(t_{J,I,\epsilon},0)})\circ{e_{I}}_{(\epsilon_{I},0)}

(5.35)

Here we use an obvious notation in which the part of an edge
e
between parameter values
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
is denoted by
e_{t_{2},t_{1}}
, the image of such an edge by a diffeomorphism
\phi

is denoted by
\phi(e_{t_{2},t_{1}})
and edge composition is denoted by ‘
\circ
’.
From Appendix
D.2
, similar to section 3 of Reference
[
2
]
,

t_{I}=\epsilon_{I}
is the parameter value such that the point
e_{I}(t_{I}=\epsilon_{I})
is exactly
v_{I,\epsilon}
i.e.:

e_{I}(t_{I}=\epsilon_{I})=v_{I,\epsilon}.

(5.36)

We remark here that the
only
significant difference with the analysis of section 3, Reference
[
2
]
is that the
electric
diffeomorphism
{\phi}_{I,\epsilon}
of that work is replaced here by the
semianalytic

diffemorphism
{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}
.

20
20

20

An additional but insignificant difference with the analysis of
[
2
]
is the difference in the detailed specification of the support of
the vector field
{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I,\epsilon}
in Appendix
D.2
and in that work.

Hence in contrast to the situation with
{\phi}_{I,\epsilon}
,
no kinks
are created by
{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}
.
In more detail, the semianalytic diffeomorphism
{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}
is imagined to be the finite transformation generated by the vector field
e_{I,\epsilon}
and is assumed to enjoy the following properties:

(i)
{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}
is the identity outside the support
B_{2\epsilon}(v)
of
e_{I,\epsilon}
.

(ii) Consistent with (
5.5
),
{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}
maps
e_{I}
to itself.

(iii) In particular
{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}
displaces
v
to
v_{I,\epsilon}
where as in section
2.3.1
,
v_{I,\epsilon}
is located at a coordinate distance
\epsilon
from
v
along the edge
e_{I}
, the coordinates
being the RNC at
v
.

{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}
subject to (i)-(iii) is constructed in Appendix
D.2
(see equation (
D.13
)).

Substituting (
5.34
) in (
5.33
) we get to leading order in
\epsilon
that:

\int_{B_{2\epsilon}(v)}\mathrm{d}^{3}x{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}{\hat{F}}_{ab}^{k}{\hat{E}}_{k}^{b}(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}=-\frac{i}{\epsilon}\sum_{J\neq I}(h_{e_{J}}(1,0)(h_{{\bar{l}}_{IJ,\epsilon}}-{\bf 1}))^{A_{J}}_{\;\;B_{J}}\frac{\partial(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}}{\partial h_{e_{J}\;B_{J}}^{A_{J}}}.

(5.37)

In equation (
5.37
) each
h_{e_{J}}(1,0)(h_{{\bar{l}}_{IJ,\epsilon}}-{\bf 1})
term is of
O(\epsilon^{2})
when evaluated on smooth (i.e.
C^{r}
) connections.
As in
[
2
]
we re-express the sum over such contributions in (
5.37
) in terms of products of such contributions, this re-expression agreeing with (
5.37
) to leading order in
\epsilon

so as to lead to a valid approximant constraint action at regulation parameter value
\epsilon
.
We recall the essential steps below, for details the reader may consult Reference
[
2
]
.

Denoting
h_{e_{J}}(1,0)
by
x_{\alpha}
,
(h_{e_{J}}(1,0)(h_{{\bar{l}}_{IJ,\epsilon}}-{\bf 1}))^{A_{J}}_{\;\;B_{J}}
by
\delta x_{\alpha}
and
(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}
by
F(x)
,
equation (
5.37
) is of the form
\sum_{\alpha}\delta x_{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\alpha}}F(x)
which can be rewritten to leading order in
\delta x_{\alpha}
as
F(x+\delta x)-F(x)
.
The fact that
F\sim(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}
is a
product
over edge holonomies leads to the replacement of the sum over edges of the form (
5.37
) by a difference of products over edges.
Using the notation employed in
[
2
]
we write
(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}
as:

(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}=C_{\lambda,\lambda}^{A_{1}..A_{N}}(\prod_{K=1}^{N}h_{e_{K}}(1,0)^{B_{K}}{}_{A_{K}})S_{rest}{}_{B_{1}..B_{N}}

(5.38)

where
C_{\lambda,\lambda}^{A_{1}..A_{N}}
is the intertwiner at
v
and
S_{rest}
does
not
depend on the edge holonomies over edges which emananate from
v
.
From the discussion above, we rewrite (
5.37
) to leading order in
\epsilon
as:

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2\epsilon}(v)}\mathrm{d}^{3}x{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}{\hat{F}}_{ab}^{k}{\hat{E}}_{k}^{b}(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}=-\frac{i}{\epsilon}\left(C_{\lambda,\lambda}^{A_{1}..A_{N}}(\prod_{J\neq I}h_{e_{J}}(1,0)h_{{\bar{l}}_{IJ,\epsilon}}^{B_{J}}{}_{A_{J}})S_{rest}{}_{B_{1}..B_{N}}-(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}\right)

(5.39)

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\epsilon}\left(C_{\lambda,\lambda}^{A_{1}..A_{N}}\prod_{J\neq I}(h_{e_{J}}(1,t_{\epsilon,J,I})h_{{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}({e_{J}})}(t_{J,I,\epsilon},0)h_{{e_{I}}}(\epsilon_{I},0))^{B_{J}}{}_{A_{J}}h_{e_{I}}^{B_{I}}{}_{A_{I}}S_{rest}{}_{B_{1}..B_{N}}-(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}\right)\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(5.40)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{\epsilon}\left(C_{\lambda,\lambda}^{A_{1}..A_{N}}\prod_{J\neq I}(h_{{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}({e_{J}})}(1,0)h_{{e_{I}}}(\epsilon_{I},0))^{B_{J}}{}_{A_{J}}h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{B_{I}}{}_{A_{I}}S_{rest}{}_{B_{1}..B_{N}}-(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}\right).

(5.41)

In (
5.39
) we have used the equivalent to leading order ‘difference in products’ form as discussed above. In (
5.40
) we have used (
5.35
). In (
5.41
),

h_{{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}({e_{J}})}(1,0)
denotes the holonomy over the image of the edge
{e_{J}}
by the semianalytic diffeomorphism
{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}
.
In obtaining (
5.41
) from (
5.40
),
we have used the fact that the diffeomorphism
{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}
is of compact support and that the part of the
J
th edge which lies within this support is that between parameter
values
t_{J}=0
and
t_{J}=t_{\epsilon,J,I}
to conclude that:

{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}({e_{J}}_{(1,0)})={\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}({e_{J}}_{(1,t_{J,I,\epsilon})})\circ{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}({e_{J}}_{(t_{J,I,\epsilon},0)})={e_{J}}_{(1,t_{J,I,\epsilon})}\circ{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}({e_{J}}_{(t_{J,I,\epsilon},0)}).

(5.42)

Next, recall that the invariance of the intertwiner
C_{\lambda,\lambda}
under a gauge transformation
g
implies that:

(C_{\lambda})_{\lambda}^{A_{1}..A_{N}}(\prod_{J\neq I}g^{B_{J}}{}_{A_{J}})=(C_{\lambda})_{\lambda}^{B_{1}...B_{N}}g^{-1}{}^{A_{I}}{}_{B_{I}}.

(5.43)

Setting
g=h_{{e_{I}}}(\epsilon_{I},0)
it is straightforward to obtain:

\displaystyle C_{\lambda,\lambda}^{A_{1}..A_{N}}\prod_{J\neq I}(h_{{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}({e_{J}})}(1,0)h_{{e_{I}}}(\epsilon_{I},0))^{B_{J}}{}_{A_{J}}h_{e_{I}}(1,0)^{B_{I}}{}_{A_{I}}S_{rest}{}_{B_{1}..B_{N}}

\displaystyle=C_{\lambda,\lambda}^{A_{1}..A_{N}}\prod_{J\neq I}(h_{{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}({e_{J}})}(1,0))^{B_{J}}{}_{A_{J}}h_{e_{I}}(1,\epsilon_{I})^{B_{I}}{}_{A_{I}}S_{rest}{}_{B_{1}..B_{N}}

(5.44)

From properties (i)-(iii) of
{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}
in conjunction with (
5.36
)
it is straightforward to see that:

\displaystyle C_{\lambda,\lambda}^{A_{1}..A_{N}}\prod_{J\neq I}(h_{{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}({e_{J}})}(1,0))^{B_{J}}{}_{A_{J}}h_{e_{I}}(1,\epsilon_{I})^{B_{I}}{}_{A_{I}}S_{rest}{}_{B_{1}..B_{N}}

(5.45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{\lambda,\lambda}^{A_{1}..A_{N}}\prod_{J\neq I}(h_{{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}({e_{J}})}(1,0))^{B_{J}}{}_{A_{J}}h_{{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}(e_{I})}(1,0)^{B_{I}}{}_{A_{I}}S_{rest}{}_{B_{1}..B_{N}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{\lambda,\lambda}^{A_{1}..A_{N}}\prod_{K}(h_{{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}({e_{K}})}(1,0))^{B_{K}}{}_{A_{K}}S_{rest}{}_{B_{1}..B_{N}}

(5.46)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\hat{U}}_{{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}}(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}

(5.47)

where
{\hat{U}}_{{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}}
is the unitary operator corresponding to the semianalytic diffeomorphism

{{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}}
.
Using (
5.47
) in (
5.41
) we have:

\int_{B_{2\epsilon}(v)}\mathrm{d}^{3}x{\hat{e}}^{a}_{I_{v},\epsilon}{\hat{F}}_{ab}^{k}{\hat{E}}_{k}^{b}(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}=-i\frac{({\hat{U}}_{{\bar{\phi}}_{I,\epsilon}}-{\bf 1})}{\epsilon}(S_{\lambda})_{\lambda}.

(5.48)

Similar to the transition from (
2.13
) to (
2.14
) in section
2.1.2
, we absorb overall factors in (
5.48
